ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e134	It is with pleasure that I can now present the position of the Business Committee on the agricultural settlement of the year. There are at least two good reasons for this: firstly, there was agreement with both the Small and Low Farmers, and secondly, it is a robust and forward-looking settlement, fully in line with the objectives of the agricultural notification. The opposition parties have a number of proposals in the case, but only the Progress Party is proposing to reject the agreement. All the others are behind it. I take it for granted that the representatives of the opposition parties themselves promote their various proposals. The objective of the agricultural notification of increased food production as a result of population growth is very central. The agreement further increases the focus on cereals, beef and vegetables and increases production in areas where market opportunities are good. Then it's important to have good profitability, for both recruitment and further development. I guess we're not quite there yet, but we're on our way. We are taking action in line with the agricultural communication and with the channelling policy as a central tool. Together with border protection, it is this policy that underpins our ability to have agriculture throughout the country. In cooperation with agriculture, we in the government parties will work to further develop agriculture, taking into account all the opportunities and challenges of our diverse nature and climate. There is therefore also a need to strengthen the commitment to training and competence. I am pleased that the farmers almost without exception define this year's agreement as a good deal, yes as a breakthrough to ensure satisfactory profitability development in agriculture. This is in stark contrast to the reality of the right hand party and the description of the agreement. I am well aware that the industry situation is not rosy – there are plenty of challenges. And although much is being directed at now, with better depreciation rules, tax changes, etc., it also needs to be easier to finance investments in order to develop and modernise operations. That's not what we're talking about. But the biggest threat facing agriculture, in my view, is the possibility of a government change. As the right-wing parties formulate agricultural policy, it will be dramatic. Large cuts in transfers and priority for larger units where the profitability is best for agriculture will have major structural consequences and lead to a reduction in rural agriculture. Then I'll point to the forestry. The whole value chain is in a very demanding situation. The government is following the situation closely and has taken important action in both last year’s and year’s revised national budget. Farm-forestry needs to play a more valuable role in many districts, including: on the west and northwards. Both forestry and public authorities must take responsibility. In coastal forest use, there is a lack of infrastructure. There is also a lack of tradition and culture for cooperation. It is therefore important to prioritise instruments for coastalforestry within an overall framework — in dialogue with forest owners and so that the coastal forest management team has a central role to play. Finally, I recommend that the Stortinget vote in favour of this year's agricultural settlement.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e155	The Progress Party is strongly critical of the agricultural policy pursued in the post-war period and continued by the current government. We cannot therefore in any way stand behind the negotiated agricultural agreement, because it does not offer any new direction in agricultural policy – despite the fact that there is a great need for a policy reorientation. Despite the fact that the red-green government announced a strong change in agricultural policy when it came to power, this has not happened. This year, too, there was a big gap between the requirement submitted by the farmers' organisations and the offer that came from the state. The offer from the State is approx. half of the initial requirement of the peasant organisations. The Party of Progress is of the opinion that a normal market-based system for agricultural products governed by supply and demand must be established. In the case of under-coverage in the market, the producer will be able to take a higher price, while the consumer can choose other products if the price of a product is too high. In that case, the prices of the products will vary in line with the market and the producers must be market-oriented in their production. In a situation that today, where consumers demand more than the Norwegian farmer can produce, a market-based system will contribute to higher production. In a market-based system, competition between manufacturers will also arise, leading to better products, innovation and more cost-effective production. This will contribute to better income opportunities for those producers who are skilled and innovative and who are good businessmen. We believe that market-based systems operate in all other markets, and therefore there is no reason to exempt agriculture from them. It is very regrettable to note that since the year's agricultural settlement was presented so late in the session, it was not possible to hold a committee hearing on the matter, as is usual. The Progress Party regrets that the government's management of the agricultural settlement did not allow the other organisations in or related to agriculture the opportunity to promote their views on the agricultural settlement in these consultations. Due to the arrangements for agricultural negotiations, these organisations have already been deprived of their ability to influence directly in the negotiations and directly affect the final result. The year’s agricultural settlement clearly makes its exclusive aspects visible to the other business organisations. We have received several enquiries from actors who feel that this government does not equate all the actors. I will refer you from a letter sent to all lots from several of the actors in the egg and poultry industries: The usual market mechanisms also work in the egg value chain. When there is too much or too little egg, automatic grips are made in the value chain to try to adapt demand and supply. There may be price adjustments to increase or decrease sales. Other measures are, for example: change in packaging format, design, quality sorting, changed location in store, adaptation of production in egg products factories, imports, etc. The market for eggs is not static, but constantly evolving and changing. Neither for consumers nor for the egg sector is «an egg a egg» anymore. Today, there are several qualities that most people accept that they are not able to replace each other in the market without delay, cf. qualities such as organic, free-range, environmental cages, sun-yellow plums, pre-cooked without shells, etc. It is a mistake to conclude that when Nortura defines that the company has a surplus of eggs, there are too many eggs in the market. Not least because there are regulatory measures that are initiated on forecasts. The error margins we see in today's market forecasting, which means that disposals by market regulators are based on these, can pose major challenges to private egg packaging. In addition, the practice of the legislation requires that private egg packers wishing to make use of the universal service obligation provide, on a monthly basis, sensitive information on the company’s market position to their largest competitor. At the same time, they are cut off from using the market regulation in months they buy eggs from Nortura. Together, this leads to a serious distortion of competition. This is reinforced by the fact that Nortura can use the co-financed market regulation to balance its own volume in terms of quantity and qualities. Private operators must always adapt to the market in order to secure a reserve. Nortura may choose to use common funded regulation to adapt its production and quality portfolio to the market at different risks. As ‘receivable producers’, they will receive settlement after what can be characterised as a basic price on eggs. For many producers, such a level of prices would not provide a basis for pursuing economically sound progress. In other words, it is not possible to proceed properly with the system today. This shows that market regulation in agriculture is distorting competition and destroys healthy competition in the market, which first affects private industrial enterprises and, finally, consumers. I would like to address the proposals on which the Progress Party is on its own and which are in common with others.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e204	The agricultural negotiations and the main agreement for agriculture are important concerns of Norwegian agricultural policy, and the negotiating table gives organisations power and responsibility. This year’s agricultural agreement, signed by both agricultural organisations and the state, provides the basis for a positive development in Norwegian agriculture. The biggest communication on agricultural and food policy is the basis for the year’s negotiations. The main objectives of the agricultural notification are food security, agriculture throughout the country, increased value creation and sustainable agriculture. The message adds to increased food production, while emphasising the use of national resources for roughage and pasturage. Agriculture is also an important supplier of many social goods, and there is a basis for many communities around the country. The development of income has been a central element of the agricultural negotiations. The parties have agreed that income trends over the last few years have been consistent with the provisions of the statement. The agreement this year provides a basis for an increase in income of NOK 31000 per year. Some of the income growth comes from increases in the target price. The customs adjustments made in connection with the 2013 state budget are an important element of this increase in revenue. Good income trends are also important in the years to come in order to achieve the objectives of agricultural policy. It is important to ensure the recruitment of agriculture, and it is important to maintain the area and food production. The Agriculture Communication pointed out that we are facing major challenges in parts of Agder/Telemark, coastal and fjords in the West, North Norway and the mountains of South Norway. The parties to the agricultural settlement have this year been agreed to set up a working group to look at how we can target the use or area of special operational disadvantages. This applies, for example: steep area, small cadastral parcels or edge zones. SV expects this selection to come up with concrete proposals that can help keep the area of special operating disadvantages up and continue to operate. It is important to maintain a varied structure of use, and it is important for active agriculture throughout the country. The agricultural notification focused on food production based on national resources such as roughage and pasturage. It is important to make good use of national resources, and it is important to make us less dependent on imported feed. For this reason, in recent years the government has strengthened grass-based livestock production. This year, the parties have agreed to increase the yield to grazing and to pasture. SV is committed to facilitating the increased use of roughage and grazing, which are important national resources. The food chain selection demonstrated a strong concentration of power at the distribution and retail stages. In addition, we often have strong vertical integration. This could threaten agricultural cooperation and market regulation, which is an important part of Norwegian agricultural policy. The food chain selection recommended, inter alia: to establish a law on good trade practices. The government dropped a law selection that was co-ordinated and proposed to establish such a law. It is important to follow up this work in order to reduce the unfortunate impacts of power concentration. The opposition party has a great deal of space to criticise the agricultural policy of the government party. But maybe they should look at each other a little bit. It's interesting, but also alarming, to register the very rift between the government parties in this area. The Christian People's Party is close to the government in its policy. But the Progress Party is continuing its policy of rapprochement. They cut the transfers dramatically and will also weaken customs protection. With such a policy, rural agriculture will disappear, and we will have centralised agriculture based on imported feed. As a result, real self-sufficiency will be greatly reduced, and we will face a situation of poor food security. Let's imagine farmers in Austland, on Jären, and maybe in some more areas, who feed mainly on maize from Brazil, or with other imported raw materials. There is no farming that SV looks forward to. But not enough of that, when the farmers move from a village, the entire village can be laid down. In fact, the Progress Party is in the process of changing the Norwegian map through the collapse of their agricultural policy. Right, as in any other context of this election struggle, is more impenetrable. It's a little hard to figure out if they're on the Progress Line, or just a little bit between them without being clear. What is quite clear is that now the Christian People's Party is going to get a little bit of a job to draw both the Right and the Advance Party into a sensible agricultural policy.

At the bottom of page 30 of the proposal, there is a very interesting table with the heading Wages and salaries for all groups of employees. Here we find figures from the Technical Calculation Committee for Income Statements. They show that if we see 2013 and 2014 as a whole, the average income growth expected for ordinary employees will be NOK 35 500. If the agricultural figures are isolated, the result of this year's agricultural settlement seems impressive. However, compared with the average wage trend for other employees, it appears that agriculture is again losing out. This will include, inter alia: of the fact that agriculture as a whole, and in particular the average reference use for milk/borf and cereals, has had a significantly weaker income trend over the period 2012–2013 than forecasts up to last year. Thus, an agricultural settlement this year that is useful does not even prevent the income gap from further increasing between agriculture as a whole and other groups of employees. Some numbers from the proposition can make this concrete: If we compare the forecast of income trends in 2012–2014 for agriculture as a whole and for all groups of employees, the gap increases by approx. 500 kroner during the two-year period. If we compare the forecast for income development 2012–2014 for reference use of milk and cattle, 22 year-old cows and all groups of employees, the gap increases by a total of 17 600 kroner over the two-year period. These figures are obtained from the weak economic development of agriculture last year in relation to other groups, and in addition, the income development of the other employees group is taken into account for the years 2013 and 2014. Thus, our conclusion is that last year and this year as a whole, settlements show a trend that does not deliver the promise needed to strengthen agriculture. On the contrary, the gap for some important reference uses is increasing, and for agriculture as a whole, one can barely keep pace with average wage developments for other employees. The agricultural organisations have done their utmost in the negotiations, but no longer was the government willing to reach out. The Christian People's Party therefore believes it is important to strengthen investment arrangements in agriculture. An investment package, as proposed by the Agriculture Negotiations Committee, could have contributed to this. The Christian People's Party will stimulate increased equity by investing, and want to establish a fund provision scheme with tax advantages based on the pattern of the forest fund. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e325	Now that we're dealing with agriculture for 2013, it's in place to stop and reflect on over eight years of red-green agricultural policy. Every use laid down is a defeat for the government, said the Central Party's first Minister of Agriculture under the red-green, Terje Riis-Johansen. There have indeed been many defeats to digest. ==References== 6 500 in total, or more than two uses per day, have been closed after eight years with the Central Party in the Ministerial Court of Agriculture. Nevertheless, yesterday's solutions are being questioned. I meet more and more people who say that there was at least nothing worse to be in agriculture under the previous government. What I often hear is that under the previous government the farmers knew what they could expect, the policy that was promised was held, and it was a clear direction and will in politics: "We could like it or not, but we knew at least what we could relate to. It has been completely impossible in the last eight years.» Norwegian agriculture and Norwegian agricultural policy need to be re-orientated. The framework conditions for Norwegian agriculture were designed several decades ago and, in the view of the Left, there has been a need for strong modernisation. It is not appropriate to achieve today’s stated objectives for housing, employment, food security and income levels, nor to achieve the Left’s objectives of animal welfare and more environmentally friendly agriculture. The number of operational uses and the number of active farmers are falling rapidly, and the same trend will continue with the agricultural agreement of the year. The agricultural policy of the left is based on the farmer as a self-employed person. The agricultural policy of the future must therefore be designed in such a way as to give each farmer a greater room for action, greater freedom and with a reasonable degree of economic security at the bottom. The left will therefore have a forward-looking agricultural policy based on good grant schemes combined with simplification. There is a need for a review of the entire agricultural contract institute, with the aim of amending and simplifying the negotiations to deal exclusively with instruments which have a direct impact on farmers’ income opportunities. The other areas currently covered by the agricultural agreement should be ordinary budget lines as defined by the Storting. This will be more democratic than today's system, take the negotiations out of the meeting rooms and give the Storting a clearer responsibility. The left wants a full review of the comprehensive agricultural legislation with a view to strong modernisation, fewer forms, simpler guidelines and gradual changes in market regulation. Unnecessary red tape and legislation that impedes the daily lives of the farmer must be removed. In concrete terms, the Left will remove the right to vote, remove the prohibition of division in the law of the land and have a strong decoration in price control and housing related to agricultural property. Individual farmers must also have the opportunity to organise their operations as a limited liability company and otherwise organise their activities as any other self-employed or small enterprise. Some words about the proposal for a representative on strengthening soil protection that we are discussing with the agricultural settlement for 2013. The left will strengthen soil protection. Soil protection is a prerequisite for our descendants to have the opportunity to produce their own food on their own soil. Climate change can change food production conditions across the globe and increase the need for enhanced soil protection. The left will therefore strengthen soil protection in order to ensure protection against irreversible changes in particularly valuable productive agricultural land. The left will replace, as far as possible, land which is re-disposed for expansion purposes with new cultivated area with the same value for production. The left therefore shares the basic position of the rapporteurs, but believes that some of the proposals are too detailed and too rigid to allow us to vote in favour of them. The left also shares the view expressed by most parties that good agronomy, new technologies and new and forward-looking forms of production will help to increase food production. The left will therefore vote in favour of minority proposals Nos. 1 and 2 of the Input. 507 S. The left will also vote for the 2013 agricultural settlement, as we have the tradition of supporting negotiated agreements. At the same time, we give our wholehearted support to minority proposals No. 1-11, 22 and 23 of the Innst. 508 S, on agricultural production 2013.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e352	Yesterday and yesterday I was in Rome, and it was one theme that dominated the agenda: how are we going to manage to secure the world's access to 70 pst. more food? That was the main theme. When we discuss Norwegian agricultural policy, that's what we need to bring with us, because why is what we have an agricultural policy? We have an agriculture just to produce the most important commodity of all, namely food. It's a huge challenge we're facing internationally, we're going to be able to increase access to food by 70 pst. And why do we have to increase by so much? Well, because we become more people, and because there are several who want to live like you and me, eat meat, eat better nutritionally, take a beer, have a better standard of living in China and India. This makes the world’s demand for food increasing dramatically, while entering a period where the climate is likely to become more unstable, so that producing food in many of the areas that are currently facing challenges becomes more difficult. The agricultural and food message, Welcome to the table, which we discussed in Stortinget last year, began with the international rug. The government first decided that one of our contributions to the debate on increasing world food production is that we should increase our national food production. That goal was also endorsed by the Storting: with our small agriculture, it is an objective that we should do our part in the global promise of increasing the world’s food production – therefore, the goal that begins to receive broad political support, that we should increase food production in Norway by 1 pst. every year. The fact that we have an agricultural policy in Norway has enormous ring-effects. In my county, Hedmark, is 10 pst. of all employed persons in agriculture and the food industry. The same number can be found in the North Tree Layer, we'll find almost the same number in the counties as for example. Upland and Sogn and Fjordane, we find it in district municipalities by district municipality. It has a huge effect, creates a lot of jobs, creates a lot of added value. I feel that all the parties are for that, too; they praise it and say it's great that you have food businesses, for example. Tine in Elneswagen, with all the jobs it creates. But this doesn't come of itself, and that's where the political differences are so big. Farming in Norway is demanding, and it requires action. And there's no industry and almost no policy area where the differences are greater in this room. Now I want to review what are the pillars of agricultural policy: Item No 1 is customs duties: our country is cold, wet and steep, and it is not easy to compete with e.g. Danish or French non-tariff agriculture. You need some kind of customs protection. We discuss together what solutions we are going to be able to get, and what course you are going to take in the future. It was a raise that was $12,000 higher than for other groups, because it was necessary if we are to achieve the goal of increasing food production in Norway by 1 pst in the long term. Every year. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e458	The basis for food production is green plants, whether they are eaten directly or further processed by our domestic animals. Despite very little soil per capita and large population growth, we are reducing agricultural land and at the same time weakening the productive capacity of the soil. The degree of maritime supply is falling. A new course is needed, where increased food safety is most important. A new course must be based on natural conditions for food production. Then there is no basis for economies of scale in volume, either in primary production or in the processing industry. If governments continue to give priority to economies of scale under our conditions, it requires access to cheap imports of wheat and soya, transition from family agriculture to company agriculture – for Norwegian working conditions replaced by cheap Eastern European labour – and subsidies from the state to non-constructed operational buildings. Increased food safety can only be achieved by increasing plant production in Norway. Therefore, a new course needs to focus on increased plant production, investments in new crops and grasslands, mowing and liming in order to increase the area under cultivation and improve fertility in soil. This must be given priority over the use of government appropriations for the establishment and development of poultry where the operation is based on imported cereals. The latter leads to strong regional concentration, pollution pressures and associated large unutilised agricultural areas. Yes, it is possible to set the price as a result. However, the other important factor in having a good and well-functioning import protection is the protection of the relationship with the food industry in Norway. A prerequisite for a strong food industry in Norway is to ensure volume production. We have. We have a food industry that is far more innovative than the Norwegian fishing industry, which is not subject to the same regulation as the agricultural sector. So this works in my opinion in a very good way. Then to the Progress Party. They are not just for a market, but they are for a total deregulation of Norwegian food production and the agricultural system. They are totally opposed to having some regulations in that field. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1277	Now we have just debated an industry where – I would say – large parts of the industry are growing in the ground, and now we are going to a case where we are going to go even deeper down: to an industry that has been and is incredibly important for the creation of value in Norway. The last time the Storting opened a new area for oil and gas activities was in 1994. For Norway, as an oil and gas nation, today is therefore important. We know how much this can mean for jobs and growth opportunities when opening up oil and gas activities on the base. September 15, 2010 was a truly historic day. Norway and Russia then agreed on demarcation and cooperation in the Barents Sea. Negotiations on the agreement took over 40 years. The agreement reached resolved the main open questions between Norway and Russia in the post-war period. It is this agreement that now gives us the opportunity to open this area. I am very pleased that there is a large majority in the committee to move on with this matter. We continue the tradition with broad political agreement in the field of petroleum policy. Despite the fact that Norwegian-sockets companies have made some major findings in recent years, Norwegian-socket production will decrease from 2020. In the period up to 2020, investments on Norwegian soil will be increasingly linked to future developments, such as Sverdrup and Castberg. Output from existing fields and fields that are in the late planning phase today will be reduced beyond the 2020s. Resources from fields we have not yet discovered will play an increasingly important role and will eventually become dominant on Norwegian soil. It takes a long time from opening an area to putting it into production. One example is the Snow White field. The field was put into production in 2007, while the Tromsøflaket, where the field is located, was opened in 1979. The field was detected in 1981. It is therefore important that we do not discuss the opening of this area based on the level of investment today on a Norwegian base. Today, there is a high level of investment, so high that an important discussion is the twofold division of the Norwegian economy. This picture will change over time, and the situation at the time when the Storing will decide on any field in the Barents Sea south-east will look completely different from today. For all oil and gas activities there is an environmental risk. The impact studies show that greater acute oil emissions can have serious environmental consequences. On the basis of experience gained from petroleum activities in other areas of Norwegian capelin, the likelihood of such a release is assessed as low. It is important to continue efforts to strengthen oil protection. I am pleased that the government in the notification announces that the safety authorities will start work to identify and investigate operational uncertainties and risk factors of petroleum activities in the Barents Sea south-east. The opening area is regulated by the Barents Sea Management Plan and the sea areas outside Lofoten. Therefore, for the oil and gas activities in the area, companies cannot nominate areas in a 35 km belt from the baseline. In an area between 35 and 65 km from the baseline, exploration drilling in oil leading layers will not be permitted for the period from 1 March to 31 August. In areas close to 50 km from the observed edge of ice, exploration in oil leading layers will not be permitted between 1 March and 31 August. The Government also warns that it will initiate work to identify and assess operational uncertainties and risk factors of petroleum activities in the Barents Sea south-east. The impact study carried out by the Ministry of Oil and Energy shows that drifting findings in the Barents Sea south-east could have significant societal implications, both locally in northern Norway and in the rest of the country. Activities in the Barents Sea south-east could give rise to increased competence needs in the region. It is expected that the highest demand will come for people with education in electrical, mechanical, mechanical and construction subjects from high school or from post-secondary schools. I think it is gratifying that an overall committee stresses the importance of involving local apprentices in the companies that are gradually engaged in oil and gas activities in the area. This is the first time since 1994 that the Stortinget opens a new area for oil and gas activities. The fact that oil and gas activities are now being opened up in the south-east of the Barents Sea after over 40 years of negotiations makes this a historic day. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation and recommend this recommendation from the Committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1341	In several debates here in the United Kingdom, I have pointed to the International Energy Agency, which has said that if we are to have any chance of achieving the UN's 2 degree maximum temperature increase target, two thirds of the known fossil resources must remain below the seabed or in the ground. Both the Directorate for Climate and Pollution and the Directorate for Nature Management have strongly rejected the opening of the areas at Jan Mayen, and we are pleased that such a proposal did not come to pass. Environmental authorities and environmental organisations also warn against the opening of the Barents Sea south-east. Clif and the Norwegian Polar Institute point out that there has been a very short period of time since the clearance of the divider line, and much environmental monitoring remains. The Marine Research Institute is very critical. It is therefore too early to decide on opening on the basis of a comprehensive ecosystem and knowledge-based management. It is also noted that this part of the Barents Sea is very productive and an important spawning, grazing and living area for large fish populations, as well as important for declining seabird populations and marine mammals. The impact of oil spills will be high and the potential for efficient oil preparedness is minimal. Think of an emission far north of the Finnmark coast, with a cold, dark and storm. Even if an emission is considered unlikely, we can't take any chances. The precautionary principle should have been followed. There has been particular concern about oil drilling in the areas near the edge of the ice and near the coast of Finnmark. When the management plan was created, drilling at the ice edge was not so topical. This meant that the management plan is lacking in terms of looking at the rules to be applied at the ice edge and the polar front. Therefore, government parties point to the government’s formulations that petroleum activities should not be started at the ice edge and the polar front now, but that the issue should be re-examined in the context of the next management plan update. It will be the environmental work that Right also demands. At the same time, areas closer to the coast than 35 km from the baseline shall not be included in the nomination to 23rd concession rounds and there shall be drilling time limitations in the range 35 to 65 km from the baseline and 50 km from the observed ice edge. The management plan has a definition illustrated on a map of the ice edge and it is based on average ice over the past 30 years. Others have been operating on ice for the past 10 years. The Christian People's Party is basing itself on the 2003 derogation, on maximum ice distribution. The drawback of that definition is that it is based on an exceptional year, but it is a sympathetic definition in the sense that it means that half the area is not opened. But such a definition is not part of the consensus of the government, and there is no uprising among the other opposition parties for it. So we can see that there is a massive majority in the Storting to increase the oil and gas extraction. It weakens our climate policy, and it is clearly an issue that should be at the heart of the forthcoming election campaign. Let me stress that the decision today means that there will not now be any opening of Jan Mayen, who was originally supposed to be opened in this period. The Barents Sea south-east is opened, but the Decision also provides that it cannot be drilled in the ice or near the ice edge.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1701	Norwegian gas is important for energy supply in Europe and is exported to all major consumer countries in Western Europe. Norway exported more than 100 billion Sm3 natural gas in 2010. The majority of Norwegian gas exports are in pipelines to Germany, the United Kingdom, Belgium and France, where Norwegian gas amounts to between 20 and 30 pst. of the total consumption. Norwegian gas exports in total correspond to electricity to 149 million European households. This illustrates the energy potential of our natural resource natural gas. As gas markets have grown and developed – not least through regulation in the EU internal market – this role as a guarantor of volume reductions has become somewhat less important. For example, the United Kingdom is the United Kingdom. Today, the second largest liberalised gas market after the US is with significant short-term liquidity. Developments in recent years have pushed down gas prices. The North American gas revolution has made the United States self-sufficient in gas, and gas prices in North America have fallen sharply. This significant change in the gas market should facilitate greater domestic use of gas, especially in industrial terms. Increasing the use of gas domestically in Norway has been a broad policy objective for a long time. Among other things, the Government of Bondevik II is promoting two flood notifications on the use of gas in Norway. In the processing of both messages, there was broad consensus in the Stortingen to facilitate increased use of gas in Norway, based on a good starting point for the development of gas-based industrial activity with our large oil and gas resources on a Norwegian base. Sargas' purchase of Industrial Power More is yet another example of opportunities for domestic use of natural gas, an exciting project with CO2 capture. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1756	The previous speaker accused the government of having a passive approach to the industrial use of gas in Norway. I do not agree with that. The assumption must be that it must be measured in order to wish for the industrial use of gas. In that case, it must be for this proposal and for this market place to be accepted. The marketing of gas in Norway is a commercial responsibility. The Norwegian gas transport system Gassled is an upstream system that includes land installations and a network of pipelines from findings to land, between findings and to markets. This system is operated by resource management considerations and is essential to ensure the highest possible value creation on Norwegian soil and hence for Norwegian society. It will always be that the alternative value of the sale of gas on a Norwegian base will be the price that can be achieved in Europe. In order for any market place to function, it must be reliable, that is to say, that price formations on the market place are perceived as proper for all parties. What characterises the Norwegian market for the industrial use of gas is that it is relatively small, that, in so far as it occurs, long-term industrial contracts, i.e. large volumes, are sold, and that there are little frequent transactions. It is unlikely to contribute to the correction of any market failure in the system, thereby making a trading venue much less efficient. Since the alternative value for gas sold on a Norwegian base will still be the price to be achieved in Europe, it is unlikely that the creation of a market place will lead to any change in the price at which the gas will be purchased. Summing up, industrial development in gas in Norway – which I hope you get, there are also some interesting industrial projects – these projects and enterprises must find themselves paying for the gas that they are worth and that they can compete with alternative methods of disposal. That means that, in my opinion, there is no need for such a market place, and that it is also not appropriate to use resources to investigate this further.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1855	This is not a debate for or against wind power, or for or against local communities benefiting from the establishment of wind power. For local communities, future incomes of municipalities are an important element in an overall assessment of how the municipality's land is to be used. Government parties point out that municipalities can receive income from issuing property taxes. Voluntary agreements on contributions from builders may also be concluded. It has previously decided that the corporation tax should not be payable in whole or in part to the individual municipality, because it is not predictable. It will affect smaller municipalities in particular and provide them with unstable income. When corporate taxes were removed from municipalities, they were also compensated for an increased share of the income of personal taxpayers. By 2020, we will develop a lot of renewable energy within the framework of the electricity certificates. The majority of the Committee considers that a reduction in a sample would signal substantial changes to the framework conditions and profitability of wind power. It will create an uncertainty in the industry that will make it even more difficult to achieve wind power in Norway. I believe there are two good reasons why more of the resources and revenues are due to the municipalities, and that they will also trigger more projects. The important point is that local taxation will trigger the best projects. Because we know from the debate on wind power development, that some areas are good, and that other areas are not so good. And we know that there is considerable resistance in some areas to development, and ensuring that municipalities are left with more resources will also make it easier to realize the best projects. The second is the principle, on which I would wish the representative Sande would also come into, and although the rent is not there today, one can look at it in principle: the Christian People's Party believes it is important, and right, that a person is compensated for the burden of deterioration in nature, and that resources and revenues fall to the community, as it is with hydropower. The councils have called up a separate field after Castberg, and the Castberg concession laws are well known. This was also the starting point of the debate that Stortingen had 100 years ago, where a firm right of return was established and where one also realised that municipalities and communities would have significant incomes. So we think it's exciting to have an investigation, to be able to look at the possibilities and clarify that this won't increase the overall level of taxation. It will not increase the burden on the builders, nor will it threaten any projects, as we clearly underline in the comments. But you'll get a redistribution from state to municipality, and you can, for example, make it possible for this natural resource tax to be deducted from the State tax. In this sense, it does not mean any more increased taxes in total, but that more of the resources belong to the communities, which in turn will trigger the best projects, and one meets the principle that a desire for more tax to be applied to the municipalities.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e1935	Today, there is a cross-political consensus that wind power needs to be developed in Norway. The left is impatient to use these valuable, eternal natural resources. It will be an important contribution to reducing greenhouse gas emissions. Moreover, we believe it is important that our business and research institutions can have their rightful place in the new green low-carbon society to which the industrialized world now has to change. It is then urgent to establish a domestic market for wind power generation. In order to achieve this, the Left considers that the host municipalities for wind power must have a legitimate share of the profits when they make their natural capital available for power generation. In addition to good support schemes, the support and legitimacy of wind power generation in the communities concerned must be ensured. Today, wind power municipalities are not guaranteed a share of the value creation for which their wind resources form the basis. Norway has a 100-year hydropower regime, with different schemes to protect the interests of the communities concerned. As the representative Sande, the Left also believes that wind power expansion has many parallels to hydroelectric development. You take advantage of the eternal energy resources in the form of water that runs and wind that blows. Resources must be used where they are located. Unlike fossil fuels, the environmental problems of wind power and hydropower are local. There is no release into the atmosphere, but renewable energy sources involve degraded areas and landscape interventions and can interfere with animal and bird life. Natural resource taxes on hydropower plants were designed in 1996 and a broad political majority supported the scheme. The left believes that the principle argument The Size provided the basis for the imposition of natural resource tax on hydropower 12 years ago, which is highly transferable to wind power. It is unreasonable for local natural capital to be placed at the disposal of power producers without compensation to the community concerned. At the same time, the Left believes that it is also important that tax systems do not make wind power unprofitable. An increased tax burden in the current situation will be a new obstacle to the development of wind power, and the Left therefore believes that the design of the natural resource tax in hydropower is ideal. The left considers that a similar system for the wind power sector must be designed with the necessary adaptations. As long as the tax is designed in such a way as not to result in increased expenditure on the builder, such a scheme should not prevent more wind power. On the contrary, the builder will be met by a more benevolent local environment, thus reducing a significant obstacle to wind power generation today. Municipalities will receive a long-term and stable income – as a statutory right and not subject to negotiations.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e2211	At the request of the Energy and Environment Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 40 minutes and allocated by up to 5 minutes to each party and up to 5 minutes to a member of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e2220	It is an overall committee that underlines the importance of preserving cultural memories in Norway, for the benefit of the community and future generations. Cultural memories are important sources of knowledge and experience and a link between epochs that have great value for society and great importance for our identity, well-being and self-understanding. At the same time, cultural memories are under pressure from several teams. Population growth, centralisation and urbanisation, climate change and society’s need for new infrastructure contribute to this. It makes it extra important to have a thoughtful and comprehensive strategy to preserve and renovate important cultural memories. There is little doubt that private owners and non-governmental organisations are doing a significant part of the job of preserving cultural memories. Very many cultural memories are privately owned, and very many cultural memories that are not privately owned have great pleasure in volunteering and involvement in the community. Good cooperation between public authorities, non-governmental organisations and private owners is therefore the main guarantee that cultural heritage will continue to be preserved in the future. The public has a special responsibility to facilitate this cooperation, through close dialogue and long-term, good and motivating framework conditions, and with respect for the independence and peculiarity of the volunteer. The Committee considers that the public should facilitate new areas of use and activity for cultural memories. Use is often the best protection. At the same time, new forms of use can facilitate value creation and job creation and help to keep old craft traditions in check. The small protective craftsmanship needs to be strengthened, and this will enable us to guarantee the necessary skills to safeguard our common cultural heritage. In municipalities too, it is important that adequate competences are available on cultural memory protection, so that important cultural memories can be preserved and further developed, to the delight of local communities and visitors. Many cultural memories represent great values that we have to take care of for generations to come. However, the value of protection is not the same for all cultural memories, and the extent of protection must be weighed against both the value of protection and economic considerations. In St.meld. No 16 for 2004–2005, Life with cultural memories, identified three national objectives for cultural memory policy. These include: the objective of ensuring a level of peace and protection of cultural memories and normal maintenance by 2020, and that cultural memory loss should not exceed 0,5 pst. annual by 2020. In order to follow up these objectives, two years later, the Stortinget decided that ten conservation programmes should be established. Some of the conservation programmes have had good results, including: 21 of our 28 stave churches have been restored. At the same time, much remains to be done before the 2020 targets are met. The challenge for the ten programmes is different, but there is an ongoing lack of craft skills and financial resources to complete the programmes. The National Audit Office’s administrative audit in 2008-2009 concluded that key assumptions for achieving the 2020 targets are not in place. To the right, this Communication does not consider that there are any significant new instruments that will help achieve the objectives set by the Storting. In particular, it is for private owners of cultural memories that new targeted measures are necessary to help increase motivation and the financial capacity to maintain important cultural values on behalf of the community. Therefore, the right proposes that tax deductions be introduced for maintenance costs in the case of saved buildings and that preserved buildings be exempted from municipal property tax. These are simple and unbureaucratic instruments that will significantly remedy the situation for private owners. It is also essential that the peace process itself be improved. It is necessary to set deadlines for safeguard measures and provisions ensuring early and effective participation and influence by owners in peace processes. The right also considers that requirements should be introduced to investigate the administrative and economic consequences of rescue proposals and to offer follow-up to owners. If the value in use is reduced as a result of conservation, the owners should be compensated for this. In the right-hand view, a model for the prioritisation and financing of archaeological excavations that improves the distribution and financial burden of such excavations should also be investigated. When the Norwegian Cultural Memory Fund was established in 2002, it was in order to ensure predictability and long-term financing in the cultural memory field and to find good ways of connecting public and private capital. So far, the fund has been a success because government grants have contributed to trigger substantial financing from private owners and businesses. Unfortunately, the Cultural Memory Fund was wound up as a fund in 2012. In order to ensure predictable access to capital, therefore, the Right proposes that the fund be restored and that the return is set at 4 pst., equal to the return of the State pension fund abroad. If we are to achieve the cultural memories in 2020, the fund capital must be strengthened in the coming years. I bring up the proposals No. 1 and 2 from the Right, the Party of Advancement and the Christian People's Party, and proposal No. 3 from the Right and Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e2403	Since the end of the last Ice Age and until today, man has commanded and lived in what is now Norway. They're a part of giving the cities a unique character. They provide a large potential for value creation and are important in a sustainability perspective. In order to be successful in urban development, both knowledge and willingness to take care of and further develop quality is essential. Another example is climate change – changes that in the short term could seriously damage important cultural memories. Increasing internationalization will also shape our society in the future. Cultural memory and knowledge of the past can contribute to the understanding, identity and stability of a changing society. Around the world, cultural memory is threatened by expansion, natural disasters and conflicts. International cooperation is therefore important and the UNESCO World Heritage Convention is a central instrument. When it comes to the administration of our own heritage, there is a need to continue working on concrete policy and ensure better coordination of government instruments. No 12 for 2001-2002, clean and rich seas. Norway is a significant port, and we are over large sea areas. Our management plans aim at facilitating value creation through a knowledge-based and sustainable use of resources and ecosystem services, while maintaining the structure, functioning, productivity and natural diversity of ecosystems. In order to address all these considerations, knowledge is a necessary prerequisite — knowledge of status, knowledge of context. The North Sea and Skagerrak are our most intensively exploited sea area. Among other things, in 2010 the North Sea accounted for about two thirds of petroleum production on Norwegian soil. Furthermore, the North Sea and the Skagerrak represent important fishing areas and are responsible for about 25 pst. of the national catch value. In addition, the coastline of these sea areas has a large population, including residents, cabin people and other travellers. This means that areas are also used intensively for outdoor and recreational purposes. Skagerrak is the country’s most widely used area of marine recreational activities. Furthermore, these areas are also likely to be relevant to any future development of marine energy. This intensive use of these marine areas implies that different considerations need to be balanced in a good, sustainable and constructive way. We must also cooperate internationally because the North Sea and the Skagerrak are shared between eight countries: Sweden, Denmark, Germany, the Netherlands, Belgium, France and the United Kingdom. These sea areas have been and have been partially exposed to long-distance transport of pollution, but the state of the environment has, fortunately, improved – among others. as a result of international cooperation. This also applies, of course, to the management of fisheries resources. It is important that we manage the stocks in a good manner that provides a basis for profitable fishing in these marine areas and along this part of the Norwegian coast. The North Sea is expected to have about 56 pst. of the remaining petroleum resources on Norwegian cap. Furthermore, the Committee supports measures to combat marine pollution, ensure the sustainable harvesting of fish stocks, safe populations of seabirds and the establishment of marine protection areas and protected areas, as well as the knowledge-building and strengthening of international cooperation in these marine areas.

The North Sea and Skagerrak are our most intensively exploited marine areas, and we have a long experience of coexistence between our most important marine-based industries. Petroleum industry, shipping and fishing create great value for the Norwegian community. At the same time, Skagerrak is our most useful area of recreation and leisure activities at sea. In the future, renewable energy at sea, in particular sea wind, can also be an important industry. In addition, the value of the marine environment's ecosystem services comes. There's more services than we normally think about. We are intuitively aware that the sea provides us with fish, but also services such as the destruction of harmful organisms, the maintenance of the stability of the ecosystem and climate regulation are important values for us. The ecosystem selection, which is currently under way, will give us advice on the valuation of these services in August. Although much has been done to improve the environment in the North Sea and Skagerrak since the 1970s, especially by reducing industrial and sewerage pollution and long-range air pollution, the plan states that the state of the marine environment is still worrying and in many ways unsatisfactory. The North Sea and Skagerrak are a rich and productive sea area with high environmental values. In the management plan, 12 areas are identified as particularly valuable from the point of view of biological deficiency and production — such as fish and seabird populations, marine mammals and coral deposits. All twelve areas are generally vulnerable, but vulnerability varies according to their impact and timing. In addition, the coastal zone has been identified as a generally valuable area. Current, planned and future activities in the management plan area need to address the environmental challenges identified and the pooling of pressures on marine areas. The objective of good environmental status and sustainable use of the North Sea and Skagerrak means that we must work to improve the environment in those areas. Among the measures, I would like to note that the government will reduce over-fertilisation and pollution, strengthen preparedness for acute pollution, ensure resilient housing of fish stocks, ensure the stock of seabirds, continue work on marine protection plans or blue parks and further knowledge building. I would particularly like to mention marine littering, which is an ever-increasing problem – not only in our seas, but also globally. Marine trash can include: harm animal life. The government will strengthen its efforts to combat marine pollution. The government will continue the management plan system. The holistic framework for the petroleum industry is set out in the management plan for each individual area. The management plans shall be rolling and updated periodically. The Government has decided to update the Norwegian Sea Management Plan in 2014. The Barents Sea-Lofoten management plan shall be updated during the next major period. After a 20-year period, the plans are to be revised, which means a more extensive review than an update. The management plans for the seas have proved to be a good tool for the government. The management plans foster clarification in a superior framework, coordination and prioritisation in management, more robust framework conditions and strengthening coexistence between marine-based industries. The work is rented by the Ministry of the Environment, but all the departments it has something to say are involved in the work. The government will develop a map-based area tool for updating, revising and disseminating management plans. This gives a better overview of the area where the different activities take place, and provides a spatial framework for the activity. The management plans also have an international dimension. Many other countries follow the work with considerable interest, and several countries that Norway cooperates with, demand information and cooperate on this very matter. It's a sign that the Norwegian system is being noticed, not only as a good model for our seas, but also that they have a transfer value to other countries' seas. The government's aim is for Norway to be a transitory country when it comes to holistic and ecosystem-based maritime management.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e2854	Last year, about this time, we discussed Norwegian climate policy in this room, that is, what we in Norway were going to do to reduce our greenhouse gas emissions and contribute to halting and reducing global greenhouse gas emissions growth to a sustainable level. However, we know that climate change is already under way and that it will become more noticeable in the years to come, whether we are able to agree on measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions both in this country and in the world as a whole. The impact of greenhouse gases on nature and society will be the result of climate change. It also depends on the capacity, opportunities and willingness of society to take into account that the climate will change in the future and actively adapt to these changes. It is a fact that Norway is exposed to a long-distance coast and large high-mountain areas that extend into the Arctic. Weather and climate affect almost all parts of society and is an important part of the everyday life of most people. The Norwegian people have therefore always been used to taking into account the weather and have adapted over the centuries to a changing climate. Nevertheless, more extreme weather, with storms and more frequent floods, could in the future put society to great test. We have seen several examples of this in several parts of the country in recent years – most recently in Eastern Europe now in spring. A modern society is vulnerable to natural disasters. Very much of the infrastructure, machinery, appliances and other aids in our society depend on electricity. The power supply system must be dimensioned in order to withstand extreme weather. Major power failure will have major economic consequences and pose a threat to life and health. The Hurricane 'Days' a year and a half ago showed how vulnerable our communities are when the current goes away for longer periods. Societal change will affect our vulnerability to climate change. An increasing proportion of the Norwegian population lives in urban areas and cities are expected to continue to grow. The largest cities in Norway are located on the coast or up to the watercourses. Larger and more intense catchments require more flood management in cities. Urban development in port areas also poses challenges due to sea level rises. Cities also have a lot of infrastructure, with their many buildings, roads, ports and grids. This infrastructure will be vulnerable if climate change involves more intense rainfall and increased likelihood of local rain floods. There is therefore a need to strengthen both prevention and preparedness. There is also a need to define overall state responsibility. In the case of river floods, as we experienced in Eastern Europe this spring, responsibility for preparedness is clearly defined by the NVE. But there is also a need to define responsibilities in terms of floods and sea level rises. There is therefore a weakness in the notification that it does not clarify these responsibilities. I note that the government parties also share this criticism. It is therefore essential that the State budget for 2014 finally clears the State responsibility for overwater, sea level rise and the coordination function in the climate adaptation field. There is also a need to clarify responsibilities in order to ensure the settlement of floods and hazards. Today, municipalities have a general responsibility to take care of the inhabitants and to take responsibility for the local flood and scaffolding facilities. The distribution of responsibilities between the builder, municipality, county and state related to the development of new buildings and infrastructure is clearly defined in the law through the rules on spatial planning and construction management – but it is thus a new building. When it comes to responsibility for existing settlements, it is somewhat more unclear. I record that the report states that the department has started work on clarifying the responsibility of municipalities for flood and step-by-step prevention in existing buildings. That's good. But I would like to stress that this is urgent. It is not at all urgent to have clear responsibilities when people have to leave their homes because it is no longer safe to live there because of climate change. The right considers that municipalities have to take financial responsibility – in addition to the insurance company – for the costs incurred by owners of dwellings as a result of having to move from their home or rebuild the dwelling in another place due to increased flood and slip risk. We need new knowledge about how climate change will affect each municipality. The allocations in the last few years of the state budget for flood and discard prevention cover only a fraction of the need for the identification of danger zones for flooding and migration in the municipalities. Increased efforts are therefore needed in this area in the coming years. Climate change is here whether we like it or not. Therefore, while we are taking action to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, we need to adapt to the changes that we know are coming. Fortunately, the processing of this message shows broad agreement on this.

It is therefore important to focus more on the potential impacts of climate change on national food supply. Work on securing food soils needs to continue, and we need to focus more on the necessary emergency stores for cereals, both nationally and internationally. In particular, all infrastructure is exposed to climate change. Vulnerability in infrastructure and buildings plays a major role in how society is affected by climate change. The message points to the fact that a common challenge for large parts of the infrastructure and the building stock is maintenance lag. Climate change will reinforce the need for maintenance and increase the challenges associated with aftermath. This interdependence and the high level of maintenance lead to greater vulnerability to climate change. The Christian People's Party has noticed that the government in the Communication does not clarify state responsibilities for climate adaptation. It is not clear who is to be the authority in key areas such as flooding and sea level rise, which is a clear weakness in the notification. Today, at least eight departments are responsible for different parts of work on natural hazards and climate adaptation. While the NVE has a clearly defined responsibility for prevention and protection against river flooding and migration, no such responsibility for sea level rise and flooding has been defined. Lack of clarification of state coordination responsibilities and professional responsibility contribute to making adaptation work of municipalities more difficult. Therefore, it is urgent to clarify the state responsibility for sea level rise and flooding. Climate change is increasing the need to bet on flood and discard prevention. We can never secure a hundred percent against future floods and avalanches, but reinforced efforts to prevent them can reduce the damage and reduce the size of new floods and escapes. In response to the Committee, the Ministry announces that an overall review of the provisions of the Law on Natural Damage relating to Protection will be carried out. The Christian People's Party believes that this legislative committee should be set up immediately, and that there should be an expert committee that can carry out a comprehensive legislative change that clarifies the responsibilities of the municipalities and the rights of housing owners in terms of flood and step-by-step prevention. Today there are unclear legal states, which have led to a number of legal cases. Some owners of dwellings will have to move in the future because the land becomes unsuitable and dangerous as residential property due to increased flood and mud hazards. It is particularly important to clarify responsibilities and rights in such cases.

Norway is fortunate and has competence and good conditions to adapt to the changes, but we too will be able to experience unexpected consequences and very critical combinations of different extremes — extremes we have not foreseen today. The guideline shall be incorporated into the existing governmental climate and energy policy. Everyone has a responsibility for climate change adaptation. It applies to individuals, enterprises, municipalities, counties and countries. The State shall assume responsibility in its own territory and shall facilitate the free movement of others. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e3230	Climate change is one of the greatest challenges of our time. The challenge for the world is to make energy consumption sustainable. Norway's challenge is to take our share of responsibility for developing solutions that not only help to realise a green national economy but can also have international significance. 80 pst. of global greenhouse gas emissions come from the combustion of coal, oil and gas. Renewable energy is now experiencing violent growth in many parts of the world. It is positive, and as production costs continue to fall, and technological development increases the efficiency of energy sources such as solar cells and wind turbines, renewable energy will be competitive with fossil energy sources increasingly. Even today, large solar parks without subsidies are being built in many parts of the world. Despite significant growth in renewable energy, it is difficult to imagine a transition to a low-carbon society without the significant role of CO2 capture and storage. There are 5 000 to 6 000 large point emissions in the world. In 2050, most of them must either be replaced by renewable alternatives or be cleaned. As a major oil and gas producer, Norway has both a responsibility and a self-interest in helping to develop cost-effective technologies for the capture and storage of CO2. A new coal-fired power plant on Svalbard and an assessment of the possibility of establishing an industrial treatment plant in Grenland will make a good contribution to Norway’s commitment to capture and storage, with significantly lower entry tickets than the full-scale project in Mongstad appears to have. Through the eco-innovation fund set up in the climate agreement last year, Norway has a unique opportunity to be a birth aid for new technological solutions that can help cut emissions both outdoors and at home. I'm glad that the opposition stands together to increase the yield from the Environmental Technology Fund to 4 pst., which will mean almost a doubling of the return from today's level. Norway is well placed to demonstrate that a climate-friendly modal shift is possible. Among the proposals that can contribute to this, public vehicles are required to run on CO2-free or CO2-neutral fuels by the end of 2018, to continue the benefits for alternative fuels by 2020, to require all new taxis, ferries, ferry and diesel trains to operate on emission-free or climate-neutral fuels by the end of 2022, and to change the charging system that premieres those who choose environmentally friendly vehicles to an even greater extent than now. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e3547	We are now going to discuss a proposal from the Progress Party with seven proposals with measures to improve the infrastructure of drinking water supply and sewerage in Kommune-Norway. It is an overall committee engaged in good infrastructure in the supply of drinking water and sewerage. It is essential that infrastructure such as the mains are maintained and renewed at a rate which ensures good quality of drinking water and sufficient capacity in the sewerage network. An overall committee agrees that there is a large maintenance set-up on the grid in many Norwegian municipalities. The majority of the Committee, which in this case is all minus the Progress Party, indicates that increased levels of rainfall associated with climate change will increase the pressure on the sewerage system. We have a number of laws and regulations regulating water and sewerage services in Norway. The majority of the Committee points out that in its letter of reply, the Council of State refers to Notification. Art. No. 33 for 2012–2013 on climate adaptations in Norway, which were also debated earlier today. It is proposed, inter alia: to set up a public investigation committee to assess existing legislation and make any proposals for improving the framework conditions for the management of increasing levels of flooding by the municipality in the event of climate change. The majority of the Committee points out that the water and sewerage service is a municipal responsibility financed by the water and sewerage fee. In recent years, the government has taken several measures to help the municipality secure this important infrastructure in a good way, in order to ensure that social interests are taken into account. If there is good cooperation between local and central authorities, we get a better water and sewerage service. The government has recently consulted on a proposal to amend the guidelines on municipal self-sufficiency. This may lead to possible changes in the water and sewerage fee. Norway is a party to the International Protocol on Water and Health and is committed to setting objectives to ensure good quality of drinking water. It has just been consulted on proposals for such national targets for water and health in drinking water and sanitation. Here it is a goal that we should be able to achieve the standard laid down in the legislation that the drinking water regulation and the water regulation require. The Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities and the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, and the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, On 10 January 2012, in Stortinget, we made a change in the law on municipal sewage treatment plants, which included: all new water and sewerage facilities shall be owned by the municipalities. The majority in Stortinget supported this, with the exception of the Advance Party. We did this to ensure that the inhabitants of the municipalities have sufficient and clean drinking water and a good sewerage network that ensures good hygiene. In order to achieve quality, safety and long-term sustainability in the water and sewerage sectors, the majority believe that these facilities must be municipally owned. The majority believe that the basic infrastructure of the municipality as a water and sewerage facility should be a public matter both in terms of responsibility and in terms of who owns the facilities. The maintenance of facilities and the proper control of their facilities are essential to have a good standard in their facilities and to avoid the effects of drinking water in Bergen a few years ago. The drinking water source of the municipality’s inhabitants may be vulnerable. That's what we saw during the storms in winter. The ownership of the water and sewerage network must be seen in the light of safety and preparedness. The need for renewal of the mains speaks for public ownership in order to make progress in maintenance, which has a large lag. Municipally owned water and sewerage facilities are provided with water and sewerage fees from subscribers. The revenue from the fees shall be wholly intended to cover the costs incurred by the municipality in the field. A municipality shall not be able to organise itself away from the principle of self-sufficiency. This implies that the law also applies to municipal limited liability companies owned by a municipality, in addition to companies established under the law on intermunicipal cooperation. In Norway, it is common for the development tasks to be largely outsourced to private operators, while the municipality itself is responsible for the operation and management of the facilities. The seven proposals of the Progress Party are not supported by any of the other parties, and we propose that they be annexed to the Minutes. I expect the proposers to promote the views of the minority on this matter.

I would like to refer carefully to the comments of the Right when dealing with that matter. But one relationship I'll take up. This sector is a bit special in the municipal sector. The VA sector is different from the other critical infrastructure sectors because the responsibility for authority and regulation is distributed among a number of departments, including: Ministry of the Environment, the Ministry of Health and Welfare, the Ministry of Agriculture and Food, the Ministry of Oil and Energy and the Ministry of Justice and Defence. However, none of the departments has overall or coordinating sectoral responsibilities and, as a result of the organisation of powers, the industry is subject to a rather fragmented legislative and regulatory system. For this reason, we believe in the Right that there is a need for a comprehensive sectoral law in line with the professional leadership of the 2006 public inquiry. I see that the Council of State has not signed up on the list of speakers, but I want to challenge him a little bit as to whether there is going to be work on such a sector law.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e3638	In this case, we are dealing with a proposal from the representatives of Storing, Trine Skei Grande and Borghild Tendeen to streamline labour immigration to Norway through simpler rules, better procedures and faster procedures. No 18 for 2007-2008 on labour immigration, when the majority of Stortings, all except the representatives of the Progress Party, agreed that the legislation in this area should be made simpler and more transparent. They also have ideas that can contribute as a result of having a different cultural background. Successful integration is based on reciprocity in duties and rights, communication, recognition and respect for each other as human beings.
