ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e3661	As the rapporteur has well explained in relation to the main features of this case from the Left’s Grandfather’s Group, the case contains twelve proposals which the Left believes will contribute to simpler rules, better procedures and faster procedures for recruiting labour from abroad. It was a unanimous committee that decided that the proposals should be annexed to the protocol, so I record that the right has changed the opinion since we gave the vote on the matter. I want to look back, towards the perspective message, which was dealt with here in Stortinget on 29 April this year. It describes future challenges and opportunities for the Norwegian economy and well-being, including the strong increase in labour migration to Norway after the EU enlargement in 2004. From 2005 to 2012, there have been some 330,000 more employed in the country. There are more than ten times as many as in the period 2001–2005. About 60 pst. of this increase, immigrants have been. Enterprises and public employers would not have had the opportunity to pick up this labour force domestically, so that migrant workers represent a positive contribution to the labour force. They helped to extend the strong upturn in the Norwegian economy in the run-up to the financial crisis, and over the last two years, we have seen that labour immigration has contributed to an increase in production capacity and capacity for conversion into the Norwegian economy. It is, as the rapporteur said, a broad consensus in the United Kingdom that migrant workers represent a resource for Norway, and that it is important that we manage to recruit skilled labour in a fast and efficient way. At the same time, I would like to remind you that until now, the government has presented three action plans against social dumping. It is important that the recruitment of labour takes place within the framework of the rules governing Norwegian working life. The Committee originally promoted a unanimous vote to attach the proposals from the Left to the Protocol. The justification is that the proposals have either already been implemented, have been assessed in the past and/or are considered to be a less effective solution than the ones currently in force. The proposers support what they believe is a need for more effective rules that, in a previous consultation opinion, Norwegian Industry has shown a considerable shortage of labour in different industries. However, the Council of State points out in its letter to the Committee that no feedback has been received from the employer team indicating that the legislation is the main reason for this. The first proposal, to reintroduce a specialist home and extend it to non-skilled workers, is not particularly relevant. The home we had was lifted because it was misused significantly and became a shell for social dumping. The same applies to proposal No. 2 about job application visas. Proposal No. 3 on prior approval, proposal No 5 that the employer should decide more on the requirements of competence, proposal No. 6 on simpler procedures for the renewal of work permits, proposals Nos. 8 and 9 on family immigration were all promoted in the representative proposal from the Right, which was discussed here in the Chamber in November 2011, and they were not entirely new at that time either. The prior approval of establishments was assessed and rejected in the context of the notification of St.meld. No 18 for 2007-2008 Labour immigration in June 2008. Instead, a simpler early start-up system was introduced, which also means that it is the employer who assesses the worker’s competence. The Impact Programme has already introduced simpler procedures for renewal. Family immigration issues from third countries now take about three months. Since it is a family immigration issue, almost the migrant must have his application approved first. Proposal No. 4 deals with the recognition of foreign education, and it was an important theme when, in March of this year, the European Parliament dealt with the communication on integration. The case is for follow-up in the Ministry of Children and Gender Equality. The same is true of vocational language training. Proposal No. 7 deals with rapid proceedings in the UDI, which is a work in progress, which has already produced good results and which will be continued. It also includes the part of the proceedings that takes place in the police districts. Improved information is constantly being worked on, including in cooperation with non-governmental organisations. Finally, there is reason to stress that we agree with many of the intentions behind the proposals that are being promoted, but that the majority consider these intentions to be well respected already. Since the Left has now chosen to drop five of the original proposals, this may indicate that the Left also agrees that much of this has already been done well.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-19.ana.ud1552e3717	I share the views of the proposer on the importance of facilitating the rapid and effective recruitment of skilled workers. Good practices, simple rules and quick handling are a prerequisite for Norway to attract highly skilled labour. In recent years, labour migration has been record high — and positive for Norway. It helps to meet the demand for labour and to carry out important tasks and provides useful skills. Today’s policy was based on the Employment Communication presented by the Government in 2008 and the aim of the policy is to facilitate the recruitment of skilled foreign labour by enterprises and businesses in a simple and efficient way. At the same time, every worker must be guaranteed good conditions in Norwegian working life. The fight against social dumping is therefore a very important part of the government's labour immigration policy. Policies must be designed in such a way as to contribute to the sustainable development of the welfare society. Future labour demand should mainly be met by mobilising domestic labour resources. Labour immigration in addition to this shall complement domestic labour force, not replace it. That's how we get a well-functioning labour market. I see it as the left behind the main lines of today's policy. There is a broad political consensus in Norway that we need labour. We have recently had a broad review of labour immigration policy, and new arrangements have been put in place, more in line with the proposals of the Left. I would like to add some considerations to some of the proposals. The proposer proposes to reintroduce the specialist family so that enterprises can employ non-skilled workers from non-EEA countries. It is not a reintroduction of what the previous provision was intended to cover. The purpose of the specialist provision was the rapid and effective recruitment of highly skilled labour from outside the EEA territory. The need for unskilled labour is covered, inter alia, by: by the Nordics and other citizens of the EEA territory. That is why we only want to open up the labour market to skilled workers from third countries. The proposal to extend ‘early start of work’ in the case of prior approval of enterprises and educational and research institutions has been considered in the past. The assessments showed that prior approval is administratively demanding and leads to longer, not shorter, procedural periods. Today, the situation is such that there are few complaints over a long period of time in the handling of work cases. Employers’ figures and feedback do not indicate that there are large administrative barriers to recruitment or to employment immigration to Norway. In fact, the time taken to deal with the case has declined, and although the composition and the performance requirements have changed, and the figures cannot be fully compared, directly, they nevertheless give an image. At the end of 2009, 38 pst. of the applications submitted by the skilled person concerned for less than one month, while the whole of 28 pst. was longer than three months. Now treat 65 pst. of cases within four weeks and 83 pst. within eight weeks. The Ministry follows the procedure carefully and assesses ongoing adjustments and changes that can further improve efficiency. We are also still asking for advice from employers on how to achieve a simpler and more efficient system. The proposal to amend the rules, so that the requirements for competence as skilled are more clearly defined by employers, have been implemented by the early start of work scheme. The employer is responsible for ensuring that the competence is good enough, he recruits directly, and the employee can start working before the application has been processed. Simple procedures for the recognition of foreign workers facilitate the recruitment process. Measures such as the establishment of the information centre, the updating of databases for approval decisions and the strengthening of the NOKUT resource situation have been undertaken to improve the scheme.

The right will have a lifting of competence in the women’s dominated professions in kindergarten and care. That is why we have prioritised this in the last few years' government budget. Female entrepreneurship enriches our business, but welfare systems must be set up so that women who want to start their own business are not unduly affected. More than one in three Norwegian women employed work in the health and care sector. They have a female share of 83 pst. There. A survey last year showed that 33 000 women want to start their own business in the health and care sector. These women are good private care workers. The right will have its own inaugural programme to make it easier for them to get to, together with a stronger focus on health and care research, to promote female innovation and to create better health and care services. 73 pst. of those who work involuntary part-time are women. This is a form of unemployment. About 17 pst. of part-time employees want longer weekly working hours. The proportion is highest in health and care professionals, and especially among those who work shifts and turns. More flexible rotation schemes will make it easier for women to combine full-time work with care for children. But it will also help single-parents and divorced parents of both sexes and get work and privacy to go together. Right will have more flexible rotational arrangements and support that workers should be entitled to full-time employment. But the government's proposal for a full-time right is, in effect, a right to inconvenient working hours. The proposal gives entitlement to the same working time as when taking extra shifts, and can make a commitment to permanent jobs with inconvenient working hours over several years. The right will have a clearer right to the same working time as the majority of the permanent position, combined with a slightly longer period of time before the requirement of a permanent position can be promoted. The right is concerned that society provides opportunities for everyone to make their own free choice, regardless of gender, but also regardless of age, function, outlook, orientation and origin. That's what equality is all about.

Over the past 15 years, there has been more than a double in the number of so-called child distribution cases, which now account for almost one fifth of all disputes in Norwegian district courts. Children’s law cases are reconciled in court, and they believe that many of the cases should and could have been settled in the past, so that the conflict had not been so acute – to the detriment of the children. The court administration believes that the most important way this can be done is to strengthen the mediation function of the family protection offices and to strengthen the requirement for compulsory mediation before entering the courts. It has been claimed that ‘a large proportion’ of child law cases are caused by violence, sexual assault and psychiatry. Some believe that it is best for parental conflict cases to come to justice for such reasons. However, the court administration believes that this picture is not entirely correct. The family protection office in Trondheim believes that such conditions only apply in approx. 10 pst. of the cases. The point is that there is a potential to reduce the number of cases that come to the district court. This requires good contact and interaction between the district courts and the family protection offices, and not least, as the representative from the Right underlined, more resources to the family protection offices. It is the view of the Court of Justice, and it is the view of the Christian People's Party. It's called family policy, and it's called prevention. I take up the proposal of the Christian People's Party.
